### S27 — S26 remediation: all four closed, and ninety-seven unresolvable citations removed

2026-08-05, on branch `fix/s27-ux-remediation`.

**O95 is the fourth appearance of one shape, and the reason to record this session carefully.**
A guard scoped to the instances a finding named rather than to the class it belongs to: O72's
root cause survived as O77; O79 was closed with half unfixed and returned as O82; O81 removed
thirteen citations from `docs/` while the same commit added two to `examples/`, which became
O85 — and the guard written for O85 matched `PLAN.md`, session identifiers and open-item
numbers, but never `SPEC §4.3` or `ADR-010`. **Ninety-seven such citations sat on fifteen pages
across three cold passes**, against two that were links. The site publishes no `spec` or `adr`
route, so every one of them was unreachable.

**The fix direction was decided by a fact the finding did not contain.** `SPEC.md`, `PRD.md` and
`adr/` are to be **removed from the repository before it is made public**. Linking the citations
— the obvious repair, and the one a session optimising for the finding as written would have
chosen — would therefore have manufactured ninety-seven dead links instead of leaving
ninety-seven dead references, and would have had to be undone. They are removed instead, with
the reasoning kept inline where a citation carried any, and the two existing GitHub blob links
to `adr/` are removed for the same reason. **A regression asserts no citation and no link to a
departing file remains on any reader-facing surface**, and a third asserts the load-bearing
claims those citations were attached to are still on the page — because deleting the reasoning
along with the pointer is the way this fix fails.

**O93 was a code defect in both languages, and is fixed in both.** Two renderers could each
print "the payment may have settled": an advisory keyed on `TX402_PAYMENT_AMBIGUOUS`, and the
settlement block's own header. So an ambiguous payment said it twice — and
`TX402_REDIRECT_BLOCKED`, the other exit-8 code and equally dangerous, said it once **without**
the "do not retry" instruction. Both now derive from `context.paid`, the field that actually
carries "money may have moved", so the two codes cannot drift apart again. The Python twin was
written deliberately: the finding reported the two CLIs as byte-identical, which makes it
exactly the kind of item that gets repaired only in the language it was demonstrated in.

**O92 exposed the danger of describing a workflow from its name.** The releasing page claimed
four jobs; there are five, and the undocumented `docs-published` is a hard gate both publish
jobs declare `needs` on. A first draft of the fix described that job from inference and was
**wrong** — it does not check the released version, it probes the live site for reachability and
would pass today. It was corrected against the workflow file before shipping. The regression
compares the page against the job list in `release.yml` rather than against a remembered number.

**O94 was S19's, like O83 before it.** "`null` there means 'tx402 has no identifier', never
'there was no payment'" is falsified by the project's own `missing-payment-response` fixture:
`status: "committed"` with no on-chain settlement at all. The behaviour is correct and
conservative — the buyer's ledger over-counts spend and can never under-count it — and only the
absolute word was wrong, on the page someone reads while reconciling money.

**Regressions were run against `290d69c` first**, where the TypeScript suite fails **9 of 11**
and the Python twin **3 of 5**. One draft assertion passed at the base commit because the
sentence it searched for **wraps across a line** in the source; it is now newline-tolerant. That
is the second time in three sessions a guard was caught unable to fail — after S25's
`NODE_PATH` case — and both were found only because the base-commit run is mandatory.

**Gates measured.** `pnpm check` exit 0 on Node v22.23.1 — **608 passed, 11 skipped** (597 / 11
at `290d69c`); size gate **17.27 / 25.00 KiB gz** own code and **32.00 / 32.00 KiB gz** core
import path, unchanged despite the `run.ts` edit. Python gates exit 0 — ruff, ruff format over
51 files, mypy strict over 25 source files, **591 passed at 93.01 % coverage**.

**CI at `290d69c` verified independently before starting:** run **#58 is 11 / 11**.

**CI and the publish, both measured after the fact.** `main` was fast-forwarded to `27155ae`
and pushed to `jaykomarraju/tx402-dev`. **CI run #59 on `27155ae` is 11 / 11 green**, read from
the GitHub API. **Docs run #11 fails as designed**, step for step identical to #8 through #10:
only `A deploy from main requires the deploy credentials` fails, with the three deploy steps
skipped. That is O56 fail-closed.

**The documentation was rebuilt and published** — deployment `e4f34a78`, source `27155ae`.
Verified over the public internet afterwards: `docs:live` **8 / 8**, and a sweep of **all sixteen
live pages for `SPEC §…`, `ADR-…` and `SEC-…` returns zero**, against ninety-seven before.
**Propagation staggered the hardest yet** — five sweeps before every page agreed, with pages
clearing in no particular order and `/operations/running/` last. Polling until every page and
every claim agrees, rather than until the first does, remains the only form of this check that
works.

**§11.3 is still not discharged, and S27 cannot discharge it.** Five cold re-runs, five
remediations. The direction is nonetheless consistent: **three consecutive passes have found no
payment-correctness defect**, and what remains is what the product says about itself.

### S29 — S28 remediation: all four closed, ADR-022 filed, and the size ceiling amended once

2026-08-05, on branch `fix/s29-ux-remediation`.

**O96 is the substantive one, and it is recorded as ADR-022.** Under `rechallenge-malformed` the
merchant takes the signature and answers with a 402 whose `PAYMENT-REQUIRED` does not decode.
That surfaced as `TX402_PAYMENT_REQUIRED_INVALID`, exit `5`, **`context.paid` absent** — inside
the band the CLI guide documents as "no signature was ever produced", whose advice is that
nothing local helps. Its sibling, an undecodable `PAYMENT-RESPONSE` after the same signature,
correctly produced exit `8`. The cause was mechanical: the re-challenge decode sat outside any
`try`, so the error escaped exactly as it would have from the _first_ challenge, where the
classification is right. It is now exit `9` with `paid: false` and
`reason: "rechallenge-undecodable"`. **The release is kept** — a 402 is intelligible whatever its
header says, settlement evidence still outranks the status line, and a merchant that settles and
says so is still caught as paid, which was verified directly. A malformed **first** challenge is
untouched and keeps exit `5`, pinned by a regression in both languages.

**O97 was two defects wearing one name.** `request.failed` was emitted **twice** on every
ambiguous path in TypeScript — `warn` from the ambiguity helper, `error` from the catch-all, same
`requestId`, identical payload — while Python emitted it once and always at `error`. So neither
implementation matched the documented rule and the two did not match each other. **The rule was
written at S27 from reading the two `emit` sites and inferring they were alternatives, without
running them.** Both languages now emit once, at a level derived from `paid`.

**O98 was written by S27 outright**, in the commit that removed ninety-seven unreachable
references: a link was replaced by its own description and the scaffolding left standing, so the
security page read "see **a deliberate v0.1 narrowing** for why" — directing a reader, in as many
words, at content that does not exist. The page now answers the question instead of deferring it.

**The size ceiling moved, and the process for that was followed rather than shortcut.** O96's fix
measured **32,819 bytes gzipped against a 32,768-byte ceiling with zero headroom — 51 bytes
over.** Six reduction attempts were measured before proposing any change to the number, one of
which (a shared error constructor) came out _worse_, and a seventh worth 20 bytes was reverted
because it traded two named constants for magic strings. The ceiling is amended to **33,024
bytes** by an ADR-008 amendment that records the measurement, what was tried, and why — including
that the M5 note had said the number was frozen for good. **The release-blocking own-code gate
did not move**: it stays at 25,600 bytes and measures 17,733, with 7.68 KiB of headroom. The
amended ceiling is the total including `@x402/core` and `zod`, roughly 15 KiB of which is not
tx402's code.

**Regressions were run against `08df8f7` first**, where the TypeScript suite fails **8 of 12** and
the Python twin **2 of 6**. **A guard was again caught unable to fail** — the dangling-reference
scan missed its target twice, first because the sentence wraps across three lines and then
because it contains `v0.1` and the pattern excluded `.`. It is now a whitespace-collapsing scan
that asks one question rather than a clever regex. That is the third such catch in three
sessions, and every one of them came from the mandatory base-commit run.

**Two pre-existing tests asserted the old classification** and were updated rather than deleted,
each keeping its money assertions unchanged and gaining a note on what ADR-022 moved and what it
did not.

**Gates measured.** `pnpm check` exit 0 on Node v22.23.1 — **620 passed, 11 skipped**; size

### S31 — S30 remediation: four prose corrections, and the rule that should have prevented three of them

2026-08-05, on branch `fix/s31-ux-remediation`.

**Every sentence written in this session was produced by running the case first.** The captured
output is below, and ADR-023 makes that the rule rather than this session's good intention.

**Observed — redirect outcomes** (both scenarios, instrumented `fetch` recording what the merchant
received):

    cross-origin-redirect  -> TX402_REDIRECT_BLOCKED   paid="unknown"
                              merchant saw: ["unsigned","signed"]
                              detail keys: causeCategory, fromOrigin, reservationExpiresAtEpochMs, toOrigin
    same-origin-redirect   -> TX402_PAYMENT_AMBIGUOUS  paid="unknown"
                              merchant saw: ["unsigned","signed"]
                              detail keys: causeCategory, reservationExpiresAtEpochMs

So the signature **is** transmitted before either redirect is refused — a redirect is a response,
and the request that provoked it had to go first — and both **throw** rather than returning the
response. The same-origin error carries no `Location`. The security page asserted the opposite on
both counts: "fails **before** the signature is transmitted" (original docs commit) and "you get
the response, see the `Location`, and decide" (**S29**). Both are exit `8`, the code every other
page defines as "money may have moved, do not retry, reconcile" — and this is the page the landing
page sends readers to. **O100, HIGH, resolved.**

**Observed — event cardinality** (survey across all sixteen scenarios, counts per `fetch`):

    request.started    1        payment.required  1, 2      sign.started     1, 2
    payment.completed  1        policy.checked    1, 2      sign.completed   1, 2
    request.failed     1        route.planned     1, 2      request.retried  1, 2
                                budget.reserved   1, 2

Three names are once per **request**; seven are once per **attempt**, so a re-challenged call emits
each of those seven twice. "Each event is emitted exactly once per request" (**S29**) was false for
seven of ten the moment it was written — including `budget.reserved`, which the same page singles
out as the event worth alerting on. **O102, MEDIUM, resolved**; the page now carries the
cardinality table above and says what it means for a counter.

**Observed — `payment.completed` at `warn`**, three reasons across the same survey:

    payment-response-absent
    payment-response-unparseable
    settlement-succeeded-resource-unusable      (paid=true — evidence present, delivery failed)

The documented condition, "`warn` when the merchant supplied no settlement evidence and it
committed anyway" (**S25**), describes only the first two; the third is its opposite. **O103, LOW,
resolved** with all three tabulated.

**O101 is not ours and is a plain error of arithmetic in prose.** The quickstart's exit-5 remedy
row said "the Solana trio", where the install row two hundred lines above lists **four** packages.
`viem` is genuinely required on both chains — `tx402/signers` imports `viem/accounts` at module
scope — so a reader who hits exit 5 and installs "the trio" stays broken. The Base half of the
same cell names `viem` explicitly, making it asymmetric as well as wrong. From S17. **Resolved
S31**, with both remedies now naming their packages and stating why `viem` is on both.

**ADR-023 is the substance of this session.** Six documented sentences across S19, S25, S27 and
S29 were all written the same way — from reading source and describing what it looked like it
would do. The existing guards structurally cannot catch this: every one was a _new_ sentence, and
a pattern list only rejects a class already named. The rule is now that behavioural prose is
written from execution, the observation goes in the `PLAN.md` entry, and the regression derives
the fact from the running client rather than banning a phrase. The S31 suite is that shape.

**Regressions were run against `dc373ef` first**, where the suite fails **7 of 10**. The three that
pass are behaviour pins by design — they assert what the client already does correctly, so the
prose cannot later be "fixed" by changing the code beneath it.

**A guard was again caught being fragile, and this time it was one of ours from S29.** Its anchor
was an exact sentence that this session legitimately reworded; `indexOf` returned `-1` and
`slice(-1)` yielded a newline, so it failed for a reason unrelated to what it guards. It is now a
whitespace-collapsed search over the whole page that asserts its own anchor. Fourth guard-fragility
catch in four sessions, and the fourth to come from running the suite rather than reasoning about
it.

**Gates measured.** `pnpm check` exit 0 on Node v22.23.1 — **630 passed, 11 skipped**; size **17.32
/ 25.00** and **32.05 / 32.25 KiB gz**, unchanged (this session touched no shipped code). Python
gates exit 0 — **597 passed at 93.01 % coverage**.

**CI and the publish, both measured after the fact.** `main` was fast-forwarded to `e664027` and
pushed to `jaykomarraju/tx402-dev`. **CI run #63 on `e664027` is 11 / 11 green**, read from the
GitHub API. **Docs run #13 fails as designed**, step for step identical to #8 through #12: only
`A deploy from main requires the deploy credentials` fails, deploy steps skipped. O56 fail-closed.

**The documentation was rebuilt and published** — deployment `1aa216c0`, source `e664027`.
`docs:live` **8 / 8**, and every corrected claim checked against the live bytes: propagation was
clean on the first sweep, as it was at S29. The polling loop stays regardless.

**No testnet funds were spent.** Every probe ran against the RPC stub and the in-process test
merchant, so balances are where S30 left them: Base Sepolia **16,048,000 atomic**, Solana Devnet
**28,698,000 atomic**.

**§11.3 is still not discharged, and S31 cannot discharge it.** Seven cold re-runs, seven
remediations — but the trend is worth reading rather than the count: **four consecutive passes

### S33 — S32 remediation: two prose corrections, and the guard shape applied to the class rather than the instance

2026-08-05, on branch `fix/s33-ux-remediation`.

**Observed — every exit-9 outcome the client can produce** (each scenario run, `details.reason`
paired with `context.paid`):

    max-paid-attempts-exhausted              paid=false    (always-402)
    paid-request-rejected                    paid=false    (refused-after-signature)
    rechallenge-undecodable                  paid=false    (rechallenge-malformed)
    settlement-unsuccessful                  paid=false    (unsuccessful-settlement)
    settlement-succeeded-resource-unusable   paid=true     (settled-but-refused, settled-but-rechallenged)

**Four unpaid reasons, and the quickstart named two.** **O104, resolved.** The page now carries
the full table above, and the `paid: false` row no longer enumerates a subset of its own causes
either.

**O104 is ours twice over, and the second half is a sub-shape ADR-023 does not cover.** The
sentence was written at **S23**, and `paid-request-rejected` already existed in the tree at that
commit — so the enumeration was incomplete the day it was written. Then **S29** added
`rechallenge-undecodable` as a fifth reason and never revisited the sentence that enumerates
them. ADR-023 requires behavioural prose to be written from execution, which addresses a sentence
being wrong _when written_; it says nothing about a correct sentence being silently falsified
later by a change that adds a case. **The guard shape that catches both is to derive the set from
the running client and assert the page accounts for every member**, so growing the set fails a
test. That is what the S33 suite does.

**The breadth was deliberately not scoped to the finding this time.** Guarding the set a finding
happened to point at, and leaving its siblings unguarded, is this project's single most repeated
mistake — O72→O77, O79→O82, O81→O85, O85's guard→O95, and now ADR-023's principle→O104. So the
suite also pins two closed sets that **no finding has named**: the `tx402/signers` exports, and
`CHAIN_INSTALL_COMMANDS` against the quickstart's install rows and its exit-5 remedy. That last
set is behind **four** past findings (O72, O77, O82, O101) and had never been pinned to the pages
that restate it. Both breadth guards pass at `977b4fc` and are labelled as guards rather than
evidence.

**O105 is not ours** — from the original documentation commit. The API page's `tx402/signers` row
described the module as wrapping "a raw private key as an `EvmSigner`", naming neither of the two
functions it exports; the omitted one, `keypairToSolanaSigner`, is what the quickstart's own
Solana snippet imports. Fixed at the generator, since the page is generated. **Resolved S33.**

**Regressions were run against `977b4fc` first**, where the suite fails **5 of 8**. Three pass
there: the sweep-sanity check and the two breadth guards.

**A figure in our own handoffs was wrong, and S32 caught it.** The Python gate's format step is

`ruff format --check src tests`, a scope chosen for convenience rather than the one CI runs. Not
a product claim, but the same carelessness as the mypy scope S28 flagged. Both are now stated
against what CI actually executes.

**Gates measured.** `pnpm check` exit 0 on Node v22.23.1 — **638 passed, 11 skipped**; size
**17.32 / 25.00** and **32.05 / 32.25 KiB gz**, unchanged (no shipped code touched). Python gates
exit 0 at CI's scope — ruff clean, ruff format over **53 files**, `mypy --strict src` over 25
files, **597 passed at 93.01 % coverage**.

**CI and the publish, both measured after the fact.** `main` was fast-forwarded to `58cd5ed` and

## 8. Session Protocol (how this stays a living document)

**At the start of every session,** the agent MUST:

1. Read `PLAN.md`, then `SPEC.md` (authoritative) and `PRD.md` (intent).
2. Read the §7 status board and the §9 open-items log to find the current position.
3. Confirm the working tree is clean and CI is green before writing new code.

**At the end of every session,** the agent MUST, in this order:

1. Run the full test suite and record actual results — including failures, verbatim.
2. Update §7 status board (session status, test IDs now green, reservation status).
3. Append to §9 open items / risk log: anything discovered, deferred, or blocked.
4. Record any SPEC deviation as a new ADR in `adr/` and reference it in §2.
5. Commit `PLAN.md` together with the session's code, message
   `chore(plan): session <N> — <milestone> <status>`.

**Commit authorship (standing rule, from S11).** Commits carry **no `Co-Authored-By: Claude …`
trailer**, and no other agent-attribution trailer either. The user pushes under their own account
only, and the repository's git identity — `Jayanth Komarraju <jay.komarraju@gmail.com>` — is
already correct. A future session must not reintroduce the trailer, including when a tool or
default template suggests it.

**Pushing (standing rule, from S11; scope corrected at S13).** Never commit, push, or
otherwise touch the public `neogeeks/tx402` repository without the user's explicit,
in-session approval. `origin` remains `jaykomarraju/tx402-dev`, and **the migration is not
planned until the §11.2 audit and the §11.3 UX pass have both run clean** — the S11 note
called it "the S12 migration", which is superseded. Pushing to `origin` for CI is ordinary
work and needs no approval. 6. **Emit the handoff prompt** (§8.1) as the final message of the session, filled in with real
values, in a copy-paste code block.

### 8.1 Handoff prompt template

> The agent fills every `<...>` with concrete values and emits this verbatim at session end.

```
Continue tx402 development in /Users/jayanthkomarraju/Documents/GitHub/tx402-dev.

Read these first, in order:
  1. PLAN.md   — living plan, status board in §7, open items in §9
  2. SPEC.md   — authoritative implementation spec (governs; PRD never overrides it)
  3. PRD.md    — product intent only
  4. adr/      — all ADRs, especially ADR-008/009/010/013 (deviations from SPEC)

Where we are:
  Last completed session: S<N> — <milestone name>
  Status: <what actually landed, in one or two sentences>
  Tests green: <T-00X, T-00Y, ...>
  Tests failing / not yet written: <list, or "none">
  Last commit: <sha> "<message>"
  CI: <green | red — reason>

Your session: S<N+1> — <milestone name>
Goal: <one sentence>
Deliverables:
  - <item>
  - <item>
Exit criteria: <specific tests / gates that must be green>

Open items you must handle or explicitly re-defer:
  - <from PLAN.md §9>

Blocked / needs the user:
  - <e.g. PyPI API token, testnet wallet funding — or "nothing">

Constraints (non-negotiable):
  - SPEC.md governs. Changing any MUST/MUST NOT requires a new ADR in adr/.
  - Package name is `tx402`, unscoped, on both npm and PyPI. No @tx402 scope.
  - Money is always integer atomic units. Never JS number / Python float.
  - Policy evaluation and budget reservation happen BEFORE any signer call (SEC-002).
  - Never log or embed signatures, keys, or authorization payloads (SEC-003).
  - The buyer SDK never calls facilitator /verify or /settle (ADR-002).

Finish by following PLAN.md §8: update the status board, log open items, commit PLAN.md
with the code, and emit the next handoff prompt.
```

**CI took three runs, and all three failures were real.** Run #29 completed as a _failure with
zero jobs_ — an invalid job-level context (`env` in a job `name`) rejects the whole workflow
before any job exists, and log download needs auth the agent does not have, so there was
nothing to read. Run #30 created its ten jobs and failed three: lint on both Node legs,
because `examples/typescript` consumes `tx402` through its emitted declarations and CI lints
_before_ building — it passed locally only because `dist/` was already there; the docs site,
because `pnpm format` had reflowed JSX inside a list item and mangled an MDX comment _after_
the successful build was observed, and `docs:check` only compares the two generated pages; and
Windows, because git's default `core.autocrlf` rewrites LF to CRLF and tx402 hashes files over
their bytes, invalidating all 65 vector hashes. Run #31 is 10/10 green. Each fix carries a
guard so the failure cannot recur silently: `tools/workflow-lint`, build-before-lint,
`pnpm docs:build` in `pnpm check`, and `.gitattributes`. **The macOS/Windows legs added this
session found a real bug on their first run**, which is the argument for having added them.

**O34 was not what the open item said it was, and the measurement is why.** S11 recorded two
candidate fixes — quantise `observedLatencyMs` into buckets, or demote it below requirement
index — and flagged both as frozen-vector contract changes needing O27/O29 handling. Before
touching a fixture, the ordering keys were instrumented across eight runs of the failing
scenario. The result rules out both fixes: `health_score` is **itself** latency-derived and
varies at its own four-decimal resolution (0.8398 against 0.8397 between runs), so it decides
some passes and the raw latency decides others. Coarsening or demoting the key _below_ the
score leaves the key _above_ it exactly as noisy. Both proposed fixes would therefore have
changed contract surface in both languages and left the flake in place.

The actual defect was the test's premise. SPEC §6.4 step 19 conditions determinism on
"identical inputs **and health state**", and both measured keys are fresh wall-clock readings
of the balance probe — five fresh clients are five different health states. The TypeScript
counterpart of the same test never flaked because it pins `preferNetworks` and so never
reaches the measured keys; Python's was the outlier. `order_route_candidates` is unchanged in
both languages. The end-to-end test now holds every key above the measured ones fixed, and
step 19's real content — a pure function, and an exact tie falling through to requirement
index — is asserted directly in both suites. **12/12 clean runs under coverage**, against
roughly one failure in four before.

**The TTV number is against a genuinely settled payment.** The public demo merchant at
`x402.org/protected` is down (502), but the facilitator at `https://x402.org/facilitator` is
up, keyless, and supports `exact / eip155:84532` at x402Version 2. `tools/test-merchant` gained
a `facilitatorUrl` option, so the local merchant now performs a real `/verify` and `/settle`
and reports the on-chain transaction in `PAYMENT-RESPONSE`. ADR-002 keeps both calls on the
merchant, so the buyer SDK still never learns a facilitator exists — which is exactly what
makes a local merchant a legitimate fixture here: the buyer's code path is the shipped one and
the fixture supplies only the counterparty.

Two runs settled. The recorded one is
[`0xe902cfa9…c30bc78`](https://sepolia.basescan.org/tx/0xe902cfa9a7c93535b3940d16af3f3d6a3e09a9ecb3f6d3518eee77aa7c30bc78),
verified by reading the receipt from `https://sepolia.base.org` rather than by trusting the
facilitator's reply: status `0x1`, block 45028561, on the manifest's USDC contract
`0x036CbD…dCF7e`, one ERC-20 `Transfer` of **1000 atomic units from
`0xaad1566216D2447B530E04945dfEefD04C84967B` to a distinct recipient**, plus the EIP-3009
`AuthorizationUsed` event that burns the nonce. The first run paid the payer's own address;
the recipient was changed precisely so the receipt answers "did value leave the wallet",
which a self-transfer would have left open. **Total 1.66 s: 278 ms to confirm facilitator
capability, 365 ms for the dry run, 989 ms for the settled call.**

**T-019's Solana leg is blocked by an external quota, and the diagnosis matters.** The Base
leg is clean: 50/50 delivered, 50 distinct `PAYMENT-SIGNATURE` digests, no untyped exception,
no degradation across the run. The Solana leg delivers exactly **8** and then fails every
remaining call with a typed `TX402_TRANSPORT`. The first reading was rate limiting, and pacing
was added — but 600 ms and 2 000 ms spacing produce the _same_ cutoff at exactly 8, which
rules a rate out. Instrumenting the RPC traffic showed why: each Solana payment costs **five**
Devnet RPC requests (2 × `getGenesisHash`, 2 × `getAccountInfo`, 1 × `getLatestBlockhash`),
and both manifest endpoints begin returning 429 after roughly forty requests from one IP —
`api.devnet.solana.com` first, then `solana-devnet.api.onfinality.io` on failover. tx402 then
does the right thing: fails over, opens both circuits, and refuses to sign. **Zero SDK-caused
signature failures and zero unhandled exceptions, which is what SPEC §12.2 actually asks of
T-019** — but the leg cannot complete fifty calls against free keyless endpoints, so it is
recorded as O35 rather than claimed. The assertions were deliberately **not** weakened to make
it pass.

One thing seen once and not yet explained: call 9 surfaced as `TX402_SIGNER` rather than
`TX402_TRANSPORT` while calls 10+ were transport. A 429 arriving during authorization creation
may be classified as a signer failure rather than an RPC one. It is recorded in O35 and needs
its own look; it is a diagnosis-quality issue, not a money-safety one.

**Two strict-mypy failures in `tests/test_diagnostics.py` predate this session.** They were
present on the clean S11 tree — confirmed by stashing every S12 change and re-running with a
fresh cache — so S11's "mypy clean" row was recorded against a stale cache. Both were unused
`type: ignore` comments; both are fixed here. Worth carrying forward as a process note: a
verification row for a cached tool should be written after a cache-cold run.

---

Between an unnecessary failure and an unintended transmission of an authorization, v0.1 takes the failure. | O32  | **macOS TCC can revoke an agent's filesystem access mid-session, and it does not look like a permissions bug.** This ended S10's follow-up work before it could verify anything or update this plan. **Symptom:** every read under `~/Documents` fails with `Operation not permitted`, while `~/` and `/tmp` keep working normally — so the tooling looks healthy and only this repository is unreachable. **Why the obvious workaround fails:** TCC (Transparency, Consent and Control) sits _above_ the sandbox, so `dangerouslyDisableSandbox` does not help and neither does any flag the agent controls. **Fix:** grant the terminal application Full Disk Access in System Settings → Privacy & Security, then **relaunch the terminal** — the entitlement is read at process start, so a running process keeps the old denial. | O34  | **Resolved at S12, and the open item's own diagnosis was wrong.** S11 proposed two fixes — quantise `observedLatencyMs` into buckets, or demote it below requirement index — and flagged both as frozen-vector contract changes. Instrumenting the ordering keys across eight runs before touching anything ruled out **both**: `health_score` is itself latency-derived and varies at its own four-decimal resolution (0.8398 against 0.8397 between runs), so it decides some passes and raw latency decides others. Coarsening or demoting the key below the score leaves the key above it just as noisy — both fixes would have changed contract surface in two languages and left the flake in place. The real defect was the test's premise: SPEC §6.4 step 19 conditions determinism on "identical inputs **and health state**", and both measured keys are fresh wall-clock readings, so five fresh clients are five different health states. TypeScript never flaked because its counterpart pins `preferNetworks` and never reaches the measured keys; Python's test was the outlier. `order_route_candidates` is unchanged in both languages; the end-to-end test now holds every key above the measured ones fixed, and step 19's real content is asserted directly in both suites. 12/12 clean under coverage, against ~1 in 4 before. The residual product behaviour — with no preference and equal price, the marginally faster network wins and that can vary — is documented in `docs/src/content/docs/guides/routing.mdx` rather than engineered away. | O35  | **T-019's Solana Devnet leg cannot complete against free keyless RPC endpoints, and this is a quota rather than a rate.** Found at S12. The Base Sepolia leg is clean — 50/50 delivered, 50 distinct signature digests, no untyped exception, no degradation — but the Solana leg delivers exactly **8** and then fails every remaining call with a typed `TX402_TRANSPORT`. Pacing at 600 ms and at 2 000 ms produces the _same_ cutoff at 8, which rules a rate out; instrumenting the RPC traffic showed each Solana payment costs **five** Devnet requests (2 × `getGenesisHash`, 2 × `getAccountInfo`, 1 × `getLatestBlockhash`), and both manifest endpoints start returning 429 after roughly forty requests from one IP. tx402's own behaviour is correct throughout: it fails over, opens both circuits, and refuses to sign, so SPEC §12.2's actual criterion — zero SDK-caused signature failures, zero unhandled exceptions — holds. **The assertions were deliberately not weakened to make it pass.** To claim T-019 in full, the release run needs a Devnet endpoint with adequate quota (a keyed provider, or a local validator); the suite should take an RPC override rather than the signed manifest deciding for it. Also seen once and unexplained: call 9 surfaced as `TX402_SIGNER` rather than `TX402_TRANSPORT`, so a 429 arriving during authorization creation may be misclassified as a signer failure. That is a diagnosis-quality issue, not a money-safety one, and needs its own look. **Resolved at S14, and the fix was a missing feature rather than a workaround.** RPC endpoints reached the SDK only through the signed manifest, which can only carry keyless public endpoints — so an operator with a keyed endpoint had no way to say so. **ADR-015** adds `routing.rpcOverrides`, validated and alias-resolved at construction in both languages, https-only off localhost because an RPC URL usually carries its key in the path. With a keyed Devnet endpoint the Solana leg delivers **50/50** and Base **50/50**, with no assertion weakened. The misclassification was real and TypeScript-only: `@x402/svm` performs its own RPC inside `createPaymentPayload`, and the catch there wrapped every failure as `SignerError` — wrong category and wrong `retryable`, since only `TransportError` is retryable. Python never had it, because ADR-013 makes it compile the transaction itself and fetch the blockhash through its own pool. The classifier now reads cause-chain socket codes, then an HTTP status, then a narrow message match, and fails **safe**: anything unrecognised stays the non-retryable `SignerError`.                                                                                       | Agent            | ✅ Resolved S14                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            |

Both are fixed. This is the S5 process note ("write the row after the result, never in anticipation of it") in a new form: the result was real, but it came from a cache that had not seen the files it was reporting on. `mypy`, `ruff`, `tsc --incremental`, and `vitest`'s cache all have this property; CI is cache-cold and would have caught it, which is a second argument for not recording a local-only gate as | O39  | **Windows checks out CRLF by default, which changes every conformance vector's hash. Resolved at S13.** Git on Windows defaults to `core.autocrlf=true`. tx402 hashes files and verifies signatures over their bytes (SEC-007), so a CRLF checkout invalidates all 65 vector hashes and the signed manifest — a Windows contributor could not get a green checkout of a clean tree. Found by the O7 cross-platform leg on its **first** run, which is the argument for having added it, and reproduced locally by converting one vector to CRLF and watching `conformance:check` report it content-changed. `.gitattributes` now normalises everything to `eol=lf` and marks the hash- and signature-bearing files `-text` so no future autocrlf setting or editor can touch them. The index was already pure LF, so nothing was rewritten. The recipient was chosen because its USDC associated token account already exists: an arbitrary Devnet address has none and the transfer fails `/settle` with `transaction_simulation_failed`, which is what made the first attempt look like a tx402 defect when it was a missing account. **Remaining:** generalise `tools/ttv` to take a network argument instead of hard-coding Base, and record a Solana TTV number beside the 1.66 s Base one; O35 still gates the 50-call volume leg independently. Solana Devnet **2.80 s**, tx `5x3f95pM…SoMEuub`, verified on-chain at slot 481108641 with `err: null`: payer 29.999 → 29.998 USDC, recipient 476.75202 → 476.75302, fee paid by the facilitator's fee payer so buyer SOL is untouched. | O43  | **`normalizePolicyHost("https://./x")` returns the empty string, in both languages.** `new URL` gives hostname `"."`, and the trailing-dot strip that turns `a.test.` into `a.test` reduces it to `""`. | O44  | **S15 finding — HIGH — successful settlement evidence is discarded on a non-2xx resource response.** SPEC §5.3 requires a successful reported settlement plus an unusable resource to remain committed and raise `ResourceDeliveryError` with `paid=true`. The Python integration test `Merchant(paid_status=403)` sends a successful settlement header by default and nevertheless asserts release, demonstrating that the green suite codifies the bug. Impact: a real settled payment is reported unpaid, budget is returned, and an autonomous caller can pay again or exceed its hourly cap.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              | Agent            | ✅ **Resolved S15b** — ADR-016. Settlement evidence is read on every status and outranks the status line: a reported successful settlement commits and raises `ResourceDeliveryError` with `paid: true`, including on a 402, where the old table would have released and re-signed. Two frozen vectors changed, two added; the Python fixture's 403 case now sends no settlement header and its settled counterpart is a new test. Regressions in both languages failed on `136edf7` first.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                |

A seeded store returning committed 7/reserved 3 received zero reads from `client.getBudgetState()` and the client returned hard-coded zeros; Python instead requires explicit host and asset arguments. The policy scope is the normalized merchant host in both languages, computed per request from the URL being sent. `getBudgetState()` reads the store and carries `policyScope`/`assetId`; `queryBudgetState()` matches Python's explicit query. Regressions cover two clients on one store, two hosts on one store, and a scope written by another writer.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 |

Python's `release_quietly` suppresses `Exception`, never `BaseException`. Failure-injection tests in both languages.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             |

Directly invoking Python's equivalent reader on a headerless 403 emits the same event. A headerless paid 402 re-challenge has the same false completion before re-signing. | O62  | **S15f finding — the `pnpm fuzz` release gate fails on a wall-clock-dependent seed, because it asserts an idempotence the normalizer's own contract denies.** `normalizePolicyHost` strips **exactly one** trailing dot (`policy.ts:125`), and its comment states that as the decision: "One dot, not every dot: `a.test.` is `a.test`, and `a.test..` keeps the inner one." The fuzz gate (`tools/fuzz/index.js:232-235`) then asserts, for every non-empty host, that re-normalizing the output returns the output. Those two cannot both hold: `https://a.test..` → `a.test.` → `a.test`. Whenever the generator emits a host with two or more trailing dots the gate reports `not idempotent` and exits non-zero. **Reproduce exactly:** `TX402_FUZZ_SEED=1345513073 TX402_FUZZ_N=200000 pnpm fuzz`. **Not caused by S15f**, which changed no product or tooling file: the identical failure reproduces on parent commit `157ca77`, the S15e "audit clean" commit, in a clean worktree. The seed is `Date.now() % 2**31` and CI runs 200,000 iterations, so **any** push can go red with no code change — observed 1 failure in 22 consecutive local runs at the CI setting (~5 %), which is why CI has been green until now. **This is not an allowlist bypass:** matching stays fail-closed in both directions, since `a.test..` normalizes to `a.test.` and never equals the pattern `a.test`. What must be decided is which side is wrong — strip the full root label in `normalizePolicyHost`, or stop the gate asserting more than ADR-018 promises — and either way conformance vectors, the ADR-018 amendment, and Python parity have to move together. **Left unfixed deliberately:** S15f was authorised for documentation presentation only, and changing policy-host normalization is product behaviour. **Resolved S17.** The gate was wrong, not the normalizer — see the ADR-018 S17 amendment. The assertion now checks the one-dot rule from the _input_ side, which is the only side on which it is observable, and is mutation-verified against strip-all and strip-none normalizers. Six `policy.host-normalization` conformance vectors pin it in both languages. Measured after the fix: **0 failures in 290 runs** (250 deterministic seeds + 40 wall-clock, at `TX402_FUZZ_N=200000`); the filed seed `1345513073` passes.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              | Agent            | ✅ Resolved S17 — gate corrected + 6 conformance vectors; 0 failures in 290 runs                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           |

S22 ran `--scenario unsuccessful-settlement` and `--scenario always-402` against the real facilitator with a balance reading either side: **both exit 9, both `settlement: null`, on-chain delta 0 atomic.** Re-measured at S23 — 17,798,000 atomic before and after both runs — with `--scenario settled-but-refused` as the control, which exits 9 with `paid: true`, a committed settlement, transaction `0x8612ff12517a32f3c39af26180e530b178eec0263d3888b8e05454b029ee9af3`, receipt `0x1` in block 45,091,804, moving exactly 50,000 atomic. So the docs told a user money had moved, told them not to retry, and told them to quote a hash that does not exist — when retrying is safe and correct. The second statement is wrong for its own reason: under `unsuccessful-settlement` a signature _was_ produced and transmitted; the merchant refused the **settlement**, not the signing. Severity MEDIUM rather than HIGH because the failure is financially fail-safe and `--json` carries the truth — but it sits in the quickstart's troubleshooting table, the first place a stuck user looks. **The machine-readable output was already correct** — `context.paid: false`, `details.reason: "settlement-unsuccessful"`, `settlement: null`. **Resolved S23 as a prose defect**: neither the exit mapping nor the JSON was touched. The quickstart's row now says exit 9 means "no resource" and not "you paid", and carries a two-row table keyed on `context.paid`; the CLI guide's settlement bullets are corrected and gain a `context.paid` table; its example script branches on `paid` instead of assuming; `guides/lifecycle`'s "Delivered, not settled" row gains the `paid: false` its neighbours all stated; and the generated exit-code description was fixed **in `tools/docs-gen`**, since editing the emitted page would be reverted by `docs:check`. A behavioural regression pins exit 9 / `paid: false` / `settlement: null` so the sentence cannot later be made true by changing the code.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               | Agent            | ✅ Resolved S23                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            |

Two documented things could not be done in Python from what was written. **(a) `timeouts`.** `reference/configuration` documented `timeouts.initialRequestMs` and `timeouts.paymentRetryMs` in two tables plus a semantics section with no Python spelling; `from tx402 import Timeouts` raised `ImportError`, the real constructor took a flat `payment_retry_timeout_ms`, and there was **no Python equivalent of `initialRequestMs` at all**. **(b) Budget state.** `guides/policy` said "Two calls, and the difference matters" and showed a Python block carrying the _name_ of the TypeScript snapshot and the _signature_ of the TypeScript query, so a Python reader writing `client.get_budget_state()` got `TypeError: missing 2 required keyword-only arguments`. Compounded by `/reference/api-python/` being a 404 while Configuration's "read the API reference" link went to the TypeScript-only page, so a Python reader had nowhere else to look — though the sidebar does honestly label that page "TypeScript API surface", so the gap was unfilled rather than mislabelled. **Resolved S23; recorded as ADR-021, and the two halves are deliberately _not_ fixed the same way.** SPEC §4.3 is a language-neutral normative table that names `timeouts.initialRequestMs`, so Python's absence was a conformance gap: **closed in code** as `initial_request_timeout_ms` on both Python clients, absent by default, bounding the unpaid request only, validated at construction and reporting `configPath: "timeouts.initialRequestMs"` in both languages. The budget-state split is required of TypeScript by SPEC §4.1 and of **Python by nothing** — §4.2's export table names no budget accessor — so it is **documented per language** rather than mirrored: Python does not gain `query_budget_state` and `get_budget_state` is not renamed. Narrowing the docs for (a) would have left a normative field unimplemented and merely stopped saying so; adding a snapshot for (b) would have put new mutable state on a money path for symmetry with a language. and asserts the client agrees — which is the only form of guard that could have caught this. | O106 | **S34 finding — LOW — the Python package README understated the shared conformance suite, and the gate that exists to stop that passed while the wrong number shipped.** `packages/tx402-python/README.md:7` read "both execute the same **67** language-neutral … conformance fixtures" where the suite has **73** and every other release-facing document agrees at 73. It ships on the PyPI project page. Root cause, verified this session: the README **is** in `CLAIM_FILES`, so the scanned set was not the hole; the old `CLAIM_PATTERNS` allowed only the adjectives `frozen\|shared\|cross-language` between the number and the noun, so "67 language-neutral … fixtures" matched neither pattern, and the claim also wrapped onto a `> ` blockquote continuation line that `\s+` cannot cross — two independent reasons it escaped. `index.js:287` then printed "`N documents … agree`" **unconditionally**, so a claim that matched nothing was indistinguishable from one that agreed. **Resolved S35, as a class.** The matcher is now structural — a 2–4 digit number within a bounded window of `(conformance )?(vectors\|fixtures)`, tolerating newlines and `>`/`*`/`-` continuation markers, no adjective allowlist, a single bounded lazy quantifier so it cannot backtrack catastrophically. A **"gate proved something" guard** (a wider-window superset of the matcher) fails loud on any counting context that yields no parsed claim; the green line now reports the claims actually parsed (**11, all 73**). The two documented near-misses stay uncaught (`M0 vectors` has no two-digit number; the `S15b`/`policy.host-normalization` line hides its digit behind a code span). Proven closed by reintroducing the exact phrasing and watching `check` name `README.md:7`; ten selftest cases cover the wrapped/adjective escape and the guard. **Caveat:** PyPI `tx402==0.0.0`'s description is immutable and already carries "67"; the correction lands with 0.1.0 — not edited, yanked, or re-uploaded. | O107 | **S34 finding — LOW — the two CLIs do not emit the same `--json` document.** `docs/.../guides/cli.mdx:8` promises "the same `--json` document, and the same exit codes"; exit codes matched in all 17 test-merchant scenarios (`0,0,9,0,9,5,0,8,9,8,9,9,9,8,8,8,0`), but Python's `error.context` dropped `network`/`scheme`/`amountAtomic`/`assetId` on all eleven post-routing failures, and `rechallenge-malformed` diverged further — TS `details` `{reason,schemaPath,status}` vs Python `{status,reason,attempt,maxPaidAttempts,decodeReason,schemaPath}`, a different `message`, and Python's context here also lacked `reservationId`. Derived from execution this session: both CLIs driven across all 17 scenarios and diffed field by field. SPEC §8 makes every `Tx402ErrorContext` field optional and the per-code required-details column is satisfied in both languages, so neither shape violates a MUST — a judgment call, recorded as **ADR-024**: the `--json` document is one contract and Python resolves to the TypeScript reference. **Resolved S35** by threading one `_route_context` into every post-routing failure site in `client.py` (the mirror of `client.ts:928`) and mirroring TS's `rechallenge-undecodable` `message`/`details` exactly — the `rechallenge` divergence decided explicitly and separately (converge on the reference, dropping Python's three supplementary keys, the one path where the decision removes rather than adds). The closed set is pinned by `core-spec/cli-json/expected.json` and two tests that diff each language against it; the Python pin **failed 11 of 17 at `d1bf595`**, green at HEAD; the TS pin is a breadth guard. No wire format, schema, or new field. ## 10. Verification

**Per session:** `pnpm test` (TS) and `pytest` (Python) must be green, plus the specific SPEC §12.2
test IDs named in that session's exit criteria. Report real results — a failing test is reported as
failing, with output.

**End-to-end, the release-defining check (SPEC §16):** from a clean environment, install `tx402`,
follow the documented example, and complete a **real paid call on Base Sepolia and on Solana Devnet
in under 5 minutes without reading source code.** Then:

```bash
# TypeScript
npm i tx402
npx tx402 call <testnet-merchant-url> --max-spend "0.10 USDC" --dry-run   # no signer invoked
npx tx402 call <testnet-merchant-url> --max-spend "0.10 USDC" --json      # real paid call

# Python
uv pip install tx402
python examples/python/quickstart.py
```

**Release gates (SPEC §12.4), all blocking:** P0/P1 tests green on protected `main`; no unresolved
critical/high security finding; 100 % TS↔Python conformance parity; SBOM + license + provenance +
reproducible build; testnet smoke suite passing twice from clean environments; docs published;
independent security review clear.

---

## 11. Release Sequence & Pre-Publication Gates

**This section is normative for sequencing and was set by the user at S13. No session may
skip a stage or run two in one sitting.**

### 11.1 The order, and where each stage happens

```
  remaining dev sessions        ──▶  on tx402-dev
  pre-publication audit (§11.2) ──▶  on tx402-dev, findings only ← no fixes, no publish planning
    └─ fixes, if any            ──▶  ordinary dev session
    └─ audit re-run             ──▶  must come back clean
  fresh-eyes UX pass    (§11.3) ──▶  on tx402-dev, COLD start
    └─ same: findings, then fixes, then re-run
  ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  only now: plan the publish (§11.4) — neogeeks/tx402, npm, PyPI
```

Three rules that are easy to violate and expensive to undo:

1. **Everything up to and including the UX pass happens on `tx402-dev`.** `origin` stays
   `jaykomarraju/tx402-dev`. Nothing touches `neogeeks/tx402`.
2. **The migration to `neogeeks/tx402` is not planned, scaffolded, or rehearsed until the
   audit is complete.** Not a branch, not a workflow, not a checklist. Planning the publish
   before knowing what the audit finds is how an audit becomes a formality.
3. **The audit and the UX pass are separate sessions with different postures.** The audit
   reads everything and distrusts it. The UX pass reads almost nothing and behaves like a
   stranger. Merging them destroys the second one, because you cannot un-know the codebase.

If either stage produces required changes, those land as ordinary dev work and the affected
stage is **re-run**. A stage is complete when it runs clean, not when its findings are filed.

### 11.2 Pre-publication audit charter

One session, on `tx402-dev`. It is not a feature session.

**Scope discipline — set by the user at S14, and it narrows the paragraph that follows.**
The audit session does **the audit and nothing else**. Its output is **findings**, recorded
in §9 with owners. It does not fix them, it does not clear backlog, it does not deploy, it
does not touch keys or registries, and it does not plan the publish.

This resolves a tension between this section's original wording — "only where a finding
proves it necessary, changes" — and §11.1's rule that required changes "land as ordinary
dev work and the affected stage is **re-run**." §11.1 wins. The sequence is:

```
  S15   audit          → findings only, no fixes
  S15b  ordinary dev   → fix what the findings require   (only if there is anything)
  S15c  audit re-run   → must come back clean
```

Two reasons this is worth the extra session rather than a nuisance. An auditor who is also
the repairer stops auditing the moment the first real defect appears, because fixing is
concrete and looking is not — so the back half of the repository gets a worse read than the
front half. And a finding fixed in the same breath it was found is a finding nobody
independently confirmed was real.

The auditor **may** write throwaway probes to establish a fact, and **must** write down the
reproduction for anything it claims. It must not commit a fix.

**The governing instruction: do not trust the existing tests as proof that the product
works.** They were written by the same model that wrote the implementation, against that
model's own understanding of the behaviour. That is a correlated blind spot, not coverage.
S12's O34 is the precedent: a test asserted something the specification never promised, and
every gate agreed with it for a session.

**1. Repository-wide correctness audit.** Inspect the whole repository before changing
anything. Identify:

- features that appear implemented but are not actually complete;
- stubbed, mocked, hardcoded, or placeholder behaviour;
- broken connections across the real boundaries in this project — SDK ↔ CLI, core ↔ chain
  adapters, buyer ↔ merchant ↔ facilitator, SDK ↔ signed manifest, docs site ↔ generated
  pages, and the spend ledger, which is this project's "database";
- routes or functions never exercised;
- incorrect assumptions made during implementation;
- silent failures and swallowed exceptions;
- race conditions, retry issues, and partial-write scenarios — the reservation/commit/release
  path and the paid-retry disposition table deserve specific attention;
- code that works only on the happy path;
- documentation that no longer matches behaviour.

**2. Adversarial test design.** Derive expected behaviour _independently_, from the README,
`PRD.md`, `SPEC.md`, the public interfaces, the CLI help text, the API reference, the
examples, and plain user expectation — then compare that against the implementation. Do not
start from the existing tests. Prioritise, in roughly this order:

fresh installation on a clean machine · first-run behaviour with an empty ledger · invalid
configuration · missing environment variables · expired or incorrect credentials · network
timeout and third-party outage · duplicate requests · concurrent requests · interrupted
operations · upgrade from an older version · different operating systems and runtime
versions · malformed user input · large inputs · uninstall and cleanup · public API
backward compatibility.

**3. Security and release-readiness review.** Framed as a defensive review of our own
repository. Examine: secrets accidentally committed; unsafe defaults; authentication and
authorization boundaries; input validation; injection risks; SSRF, path traversal, and
insecure file handling; dependency risks; overly broad permissions; sensitive information in
logs; rate limiting and abuse scenarios; supply-chain and package-publishing configuration;
license and attribution; and whether the examples encourage insecure usage.

**4. Maintainer-quality review.** A project can be correct and still be painful to
contribute to. Ask whether an unfamiliar developer could install it without help, understand
the architecture, run the tests locally, add a feature safely, diagnose a common error,
submit a useful issue, contribute without learning undocumented conventions, and upgrade
without breaking their system. Cover: README, quick start, architecture documentation,
configuration reference, contribution guide, code of conduct, security policy, license,
changelog, versioning policy, release process, example applications, and error messages.

### 11.3 Fresh-eyes UX pass charter

One session, on `tx402-dev`, and the **only** session in this plan that does not begin by
reading `PLAN.md`.

**Start cold, deliberately.** Read what a new user reads — the README and the documentation
site — and nothing else. Do not open `PLAN.md`, `SPEC.md`, `PRD.md`, the ADRs, or the source
until the pass is finished. The point is to find what the documentation fails to say, and
that is unrecoverable once the codebase has been read.

Then **actually use the product**: install both packages as a user would, follow the
quickstart end to end, make a real settled testnet payment on both networks, run the CLI,
run the examples, and try to do something the docs do not explicitly cover. Record every
place reality and documentation diverge, every moment of confusion, and the real
time-to-value — a stopwatch, not an estimate, against SPEC §16's five minutes.

This is the last pass before publication.

### 11.4 Publishing (planned only after §11.2 and §11.3 are clean)

Not to be designed before then. It will cover the `neogeeks/tx402` migration, npm and PyPI
trusted publishing with provenance (O10), the rotated release signing key (O12), and the
SPEC §12.4 release gates. Until the two stages above run clean, the correct amount of
publish planning is none.

**One task is already known to belong to this stage, and is recorded here so it cannot be
lost between sessions (O63, decided by the user at S17).** Every URL in this repository that
points at `neogeeks/tx402` was deliberately **left unchanged** through S17, because the
public repository is not to be touched until after the §11.3 re-run, and because the layout
of that repository — including whether `PLAN.md`, `PRD.md` and `SPEC.md` ship at all — is to
be designed only once the re-run is clean. Repointing URLs before that decision would be
work done twice against a tree that does not exist yet.

So, **after the fresh-eyes re-run passes, and as part of reformatting and pushing to the
official GitHub, every documentation link must be re-verified against the populated public
repository.** Not spot-checked — re-verified. There are thirteen references, and they fail in
two different ways:

- **Seven content links** resolve only if the pushed tree actually contains the file at that
  path: the quickstart's `git clone` and its `examples/` tree link, `security/index.mdx`'s
  ADR-014 blob link, `operations/releasing.mdx`'s `VERSIONING.md` link, `SECURITY.md`'s own
  blob link, `CONTRIBUTING.md`'s clone line, and `CHANGELOG.md`'s `[Unreleased]` link. If the
  repo-design step removes or moves a file, the link that names it breaks silently.
- **Six endpoint links** — the O3-declared security-advisory and issues URLs in
  `packages/tx402/src/meta.ts`, `packages/tx402-python/src/tx402/meta.py`, `SECURITY.md`,
  `README.md`, and `security/index.mdx` — require the repository to exist, be public, and have
  Private Vulnerability Reporting enabled. Two of those files are **compiled into the
  published packages**, so correcting them after publish is a release, not an edit.

`tools/docs-live` checks a served site, not the correctness of outbound GitHub links, so
nothing in the current gate set would catch any of this. O63 does not close until this
re-verification has been done against the real public repository.
